  **(a) The two CSVs agree. Closed.** `training_plans_inventory.csv` and `plan_performance.csv` both show 303 published, zero disagreements, zero rows missing from the inventory. The Aug 10 correction held. *(The 15 extra rows in `plan_performance.csv` are retired plans kept so lifetime sales stay complete — 23 units, $592 — all `in_inventory=FALSE`, none published. Not a defect; noted so it isn't re-raised.)*
  **(b) Root cause found for why four staleness cases went undetected: `check-plan-links.py` filtered on `is_published != TRUE` and skipped the rest.** The flag was the checker's own input, so it could only ever confirm the TRUE set — and three of the four known cases were FALSE-but-live. **This is the warning in this very item ("do not verify the inventory against a file derived from the inventory") existing in code, where the derivation was the filter on line one.** Fixed: `--audit` mode checks unpublished plans too, reports both directions, writes a separate report and deliberately leaves `plan_link_status.json` untouched so an audit run cannot alter what the build believes.
  **(c) Five plans are provably mis-flagged, found without any network call.** `plan_sales.csv` is independent of the inventory — transactions come from TrainingPeaks payouts — so *a plan cannot have sold without having been published at the time of the sale.* Five PT triathlon plans are `FALSE` with sales as recent as **2026-07-30**, thirteen days ago: `567302`, `567303`, `567304`, `567305`, `567560`. A sixth, `567561`, has no sales but completes the same ladder and sits in the same state; `567564` is its Spanish sibling. **These are the "PT triathlon siblings" named above as a confirmed staleness case — identified previously and never actually flipped.** Publishing them takes PT from 29 to 35 (+21%) and restores a complete beginner ladder (Sprint 12wk → Olympic 16wk → Middle 18wk → Full 21wk) in the thinnest catalogue. *Iván to confirm in TrainingPeaks and flip the flag in **both** CSVs.*
  **(c2) Resolved same day, and bigger than expected.** Iván filtered his own spreadsheet on unpublished, checked TrainingPeaks, and confirmed **24 plans were live all along** — not five. Flipped in both CSVs. **Catalogue 303 → 327; Portuguese 29 → 52, up 79%.** *The five sales-derived suspects were a subset of a much larger set, which is the honest lesson about the method: the sales join gives certainty about the plans that have sold and says nothing at all about the ones that haven't. It found 5 of 24. Iván's "filter the column and look" found all of them in a couple of minutes. **A clever test that covers 20% is not better than a dull one that covers 100%** — it is a way of narrowing the list, not of replacing the check.*
  **(d) The crawl ran twice and found nothing that wasn't already known.** Run 1 (pre-flip): **78 of 78** unpublished plans returned HTTP 200. Run 2 (post-flip): **54 of 54.** Zero dead, zero unresolved, both times. **Run 2's 54 are exactly the 51 race plans Iván deleted plus the 3 rows already flagged for a decision** — a perfect overlap with what he found by filtering a column, and no new information in either direction.
  ***The reason is sharper than "TrainingPeaks keeps old pages up", and I overstated it in the first version of this note.*** *I do not know whether those race plans are still purchasable on TrainingPeaks, and it does not matter, because* **`is_published` in this CSV is not purely a TrainingPeaks fact — it is partly a business decision about what the website should list.** *The race-stamped plans may well be perfectly alive on TP; the decision is that Triaperformance no longer sells them.* **No crawler can answer a question that is half a decision.** *That is the real ceiling on this approach, and it is not fixable by crawling harder.*
  **What this leaves.** `is_published` has exactly two reliable sources: the **sales join** (certain, but only speaks about plans that have sold — it found 5 of 24) and **Iván looking at TrainingPeaks and deciding** (slow, total, and the thing that actually resolved it, twice). `--audit` keeps real value in the *other* direction — `TRUE`-but-404 is a genuine dead link a customer can hit, and that does discriminate. **Do not re-run it to find unpublished-but-live plans; it structurally cannot distinguish them.**
  *`data/plan_publish_audit.json` holds run 2 and is stale: of its 54 rows, 51 are deleted, 1 published, 2 removed. Regenerate rather than read.*
  ***All 52 race-dated plans were unpublished and every one was for a 2026 race already run — correct, not a finding. 51 of them were deleted from the inventory later the same day*** (see `training-plans-analysis.md` §3).
  *Method note worth keeping: run the sales join before the crawl. It is instant, needs no network, and yields near-certainty; a live URL only proves a page resolves, a sale proves money changed hands.*

  *Original entry, retained for the reasoning that fired the trigger:* The flag has now shown confirmed staleness in both directions **four** times (Lima, PT triathlon siblings, `612978`, and the two EN Ironman plans), and the stated trigger was "when the EN Ironman check turns up a third case." It did. One systematic pass, before more facets get built on top of a flag that is wrong roughly monthly: reconcile every row in `data/training_plans_inventory.csv` against what TrainingPeaks actually serves, and reconcile the `is_published` column between the inventory and `data/plan_performance.csv` — the second copy has already drifted once. **Do not verify the inventory against a file derived from the inventory**; that only ever confirms itself, which is exactly how the Rio plan survived the Aug 6 sweep. Detail: `race-page-data-schema.md`.

##### Opened by the Gemini model migration (August 17, 2026)

*Technical record: `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` §33 and its September 4, 2026 addendum.*

**Hermes and the research agent run `gemini-3.8-flash`** (`gemini-3.7-flash` from August 17 to September 4, 2026). **The writer, the translator and the n8n intake briefing use `gemini-3.1-pro-preview` — Iván's decision, September 4, 2026, and it is settled rather than pending.** *Open from August 17 across three Flash releases as "draft both and read the Spanish"; closed by a call instead of by a test.* ⚠️ **Do not re-raise it on the next Flash release.** *What would legitimately re-open it: Google publishing multilingual or prose evaluation for the Flash line, or `gemini-3.1-pro-preview` ceasing to answer. Closing note in `open-loops-archive.md`, and in `writer_agent.py`'s docstring, which is where a session actually looks.*

*Two lines corrected here September 4, 2026, both stale rather than wrong: this paragraph read "nothing is deployed yet" (deployed August 17–24, confirmed by §14's Aug 24 note) and "all five items below" (one remained open). Neither recorded a belief that turned out false, so neither is kept struck through.*

- [ ] **Watch the first deploy after that push.** *(Opened September 4, 2026.)* The 6am run should print `hermes config.yaml unchanged` — **not** `updated and containers restarted`. *A restart on the first run means the repo copy and the live file already differ, i.e. the `scp` picked up something stale or the dashboard rewrote the file in between; the backup it takes first is what makes that recoverable.* **`cmp` is byte-level, so if the dashboard ever rewrites `config.yaml` with reordered keys the two will differ every day and Hermes will restart every morning.** *If that starts happening, the fix is to compare parsed content rather than bytes — not to remove the sync.*

- [ ] **Cron pin drift check — verify it prints, then leave it alone.** *(Opened September 4, 2026.)* `deploy-website.sh` now reports every Hermes cron job's pin against `model.default` each morning. **Deliberately a report and not a sync**: `~/.hermes/cron/jobs.json` is rewritten by the dashboard whenever a job is created or edited, so tracking it would fight the UI daily for no gain. ***What actually hurt was never the file being untracked — it was a pin going stale silently.*** *An agent-mode job pinned to a superseded model fails **closed**, which is the safe direction and also the invisible one: on August 24 that cost a Monday coaching check-in, announced as one line in Telegram among other traffic.* **It cannot fail the deploy** — instrumentation must not be able to kill the thing it measures (§33's `log_usage()` rule). *`no_agent` jobs are reported as immune rather than as unpinned, because they are.*

- [ ] **Check `model_usage` for a thinking-token regression on 3.8, after two or three Mondays.** *(Opened September 4, 2026.)* 3.8 is priced identically to 3.7 on both sides, **but it defaults to reasoning level "medium" and thinking bills at the output rate** — so the same job can cost more at the same rate. *This is the first real question the August 30 `model_usage` build exists to answer, and the column that answers it (`thinking_tokens`, stored separately because `in + out ≠ total` on 3.x) was added for exactly this.* Group by model where `caller = 'research'` and compare `thinking_tokens / output_tokens` across the 3.7 and 3.8 rows. **Needs a few real runs before it can say anything** — the research agent runs Mondays only, one call.

##### Small slot: CoachMatch greeting name (opened September 4, 2026)

**The first WhatsApp message was addressing leads as "Hola Paula Maria Gonzalez".** The parser popped only the last token as the surname, so every given name past the first stayed in `firstname` — correct on a two-token name, wrong on the Latin two-surname norm. Iván had been catching them by hand before sending. Fixed to *first non-initial token = `firstname`*, plus title-casing of SHOUTED/lowercase entries and a guard for the single-token name that used to produce an **empty** `firstname` and a bare "Hola ". Full record: `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` §8, dated note.

**Part 1 (the lead workflow) is DONE — pasted, tested and confirmed working by Iván, September 4, 2026.** Two things opened while writing the backfill, both still open:

Decide whether to delete them or move them to a terminal status.* **Found by reading a backfill dry run, which is the only thing that has ever listed the whole table row by row.** ✅ *Iván confirmed September 5, 2026: **still in Twenty** — so the 292 → 290 people-count drop that day was two other records, cause unknown.*

***The transferable half is where the fix had to go.*** There are four greetings on a CoachMatch lead and only one of them is the one Iván sees. WhatsApp 1 and email 1 read the parse; emails 2 and 3 read `person.name.firstName` from Twenty two and three days later. **Patching the message he noticed would have left the two he doesn't watch still wrong** — the value had to be fixed where it is stored, not where it was seen.

##### Opened by the pixel plan_id diagnosis (September 5, 2026)

- [ ] **`audit-runtime-paths.sh` cannot see database objects, and that is how this one hid.** *`plan_views_clean` lived only on the box from July until September 5, outside the repo, with five defects in it — including a claim in NEXT #2 that the runtime contradicted. §18's audit exists precisely to catch "the runtime is not the repo copy", and it could never have caught this: it walks **files** — cron lines, compose contexts, dispatchers, symlinks — and a Postgres view is not a file.* 🚨 ***Every §18 instance so far has been code on disk, so the control was built to the shape of the last five failures and is blind to this one.*** **A check scoped to what broke last time only catches what broke last time — this repo's own words, arriving inside the check written to prevent it.** *The fix is small: dump `pg_get_viewdef` for every view in `analytics-postgres` and diff against the repo's `schema_*.sql`, failing on anything defined only on the box. Same for the `content` and `members` databases, which have never been audited either.* ⚠️ **Scope it before building — `schema_analytics.sql` declares 4 tables and 5 views and nobody has checked that the box matches it.**

- [ ] **Diagnose the retention drop before spending the next quarter on acquisition.** **The book has shrunk in 5 of the last 6 months — 53 active at end of February, 37 today; 26 churns since April 1 against 13 signups.** And it is not only slower acquisition: retention at twelve months by signup cohort is **2024-H2 73% → 2025-H1 30% → 2025-H2 17%**, measured at fixed horizons so young cohorts aren't penalised. Full analysis, caveats and method: `tenure-analysis.md` §3.
  **Why this is placed above the growth items rather than beside them:** #1 (race pages), #3 (re-engagement blast) and #10 (referrals) all fill a bucket. **This says the bucket is leaking faster than any of them fills it** — at the current rate the book loses ~4 athletes a month, which is more than the referral program could plausibly add. *An acquisition push into a retention problem buys you the same revenue twice at twice the cost.*
  ***Cause: answered same day, and the diagnosis half of this item is closed.*** *Iván took a COO role at a SaaS startup in January 2026, which cut deliverability; the wave starts in April, one training block later — the lag you'd expect, since an athlete quits when the block they already paid for ends, not the week service thins. **Not the price migration.** He is now full-time on the business. Detail: `tenure-analysis.md` §3.*
  ***And the headline overstates it, which he was right to push on.*** *Counting revenue instead of logos: **−30% heads but −21% MRR**, because the athletes leaving are on $75–99 and the ones arriving are on $149. Average rate $93 → $105, and **revenue per coaching hour is up 13%** — which for a business constrained by his calendar is the number that matters most. **Where it stops working: break-even is 3.4 signups/month at $149 against a recent run rate of 2–3.** The gap is one signup a month, and the mix shift is self-limiting — it works only while $99 athletes are being replaced, and ends when the book is all $149.*
  **What stays open is the measurement, not the diagnosis:** `monthly_rate` (§3's revenue view is a model, not a measurement), **one row per billing period** (the name-derived `athlete_id` merges them automatically), and **a churn reason** — *Iván's point that some churn is an athlete completing their goal and resting is right, and nothing in the data can currently see it.* **Folded into the monthly-close item above rather than run as a separate export.**
  **What remains a real decision: whether the next quarter goes to retention or acquisition.** Trigger: two monthly closes, i.e. end of September.

- [ ] **#4 · Workspace DKIM — verify it is actually signing, then decide DMARC.** *(Opened September 8, 2026, as the HubSpot DNS cleanup closed. Not a HubSpot item; it is the hole that cleanup left.)* The two `hs*-_domainkey` CNAMEs were the zone's **only** DKIM records, and Workspace DKIM had never been configured — so between deleting them and adding `google._domainkey`, the domain signed nothing. **The key is now in the zone (2048-bit, verified in the Sept 8 10:35 export).** ⚠️ ***The record existing is not the same as Google signing with it*** — that needs *Start authentication* clicked in Workspace Admin, and a TXT record with the button unpressed looks identical in a zone export. **Definition of done: one mail sent through the real n8n + Gmail SMTP path (not the Gmail web UI, which is a different sender) landing in a Gmail inbox with `dkim=pass header.d=triaperformance.com` in Show original.** *Gates the #3 blast — unsigned cold mail to ~2,000 three-year-old contacts is a spam-folder outcome whatever the copy says.* **`_dmarc` stays `p=none` until DKIM has signed for a couple of weeks**; moving it to `quarantine` is a separate decision and not this item. Detail: `deploy-runbook.md` §1.

  ***Status, September 8, 2026.*** *First* Start authentication *press returned Google's red "wait up to 48 hours" error; the retry was accepted the same day.* ⚠️ **That message is ambiguous and usually is NOT about waiting** — *its common real causes are a malformed or UI-mangled TXT value, or GoDaddy appending the domain to a host that already carries it (`google._domainkey.triaperformance.com.triaperformance.com`).* ✅ ***Both were ruled out from the zone export before waiting on it*** *— the record is `google._domainkey`, `v=DKIM1; k=rsa;`, 2048-bit, correctly split into two quoted strings — so the wait was genuine propagation, which is the benign reading of that error and the only one that resolves on its own.* **Confirmed live the same day: `google._domainkey.triaperformance.com` resolves publicly (Google DoH, `Status: 0`, TTL 3600).** *The trap to avoid while waiting is regenerating the key, which restarts the clock.*

  🚦 ***What is still NOT proven, and it is the only part that matters for #3:*** *a propagated key and an accepted* Start authentication *both stop short of showing that outbound mail is actually signed.* **Definition of done is unchanged: one message through the real n8n + Gmail SMTP path, opened in Gmail, Show original, showing `dkim=pass header.d=triaperformance.com`.** *The Gmail web UI is a different sender and does not test the path the blast will use.* *Same shape as this repo's recurring defect — every intermediate check reported success and none of them looked at the artifact.*
- [ ] **#5 · Plan-completion nurture — re-target buyers when their plan ends.** *(Iván, Aug 6, 2026.)* Today a plan buyer is effectively a one-time customer: **6.4% ever buy again**, and those who do come back after a **median of 72 days** — roughly the length of the block they just finished. Nobody is asked what comes next. The mechanism: on purchase we know the plan's length, so `purchase_date + weeks - 1 week` is the moment to make contact. One rule covers most of the catalogue — 8, 12 and 16 weeks account for 168 of 237 purchases. Message should be the honest coach's question (how did it go, what's the next goal) leading to the logical next plan or All-Access, not a discount blast. **Sizing, from `training-plans-analysis.md` §10:** only 11 buyers are currently mid-plan or about to finish, but **74 finished within the last year and were never asked** — a one-off back-catalogue send, then an evergreen trigger on every future buyer. Trigger: after the HubSpot migration, which supplies the audience.
  ***A second, cheaper audience mechanism — Iván, August 31, 2026. PARKED, logged so it stops being re-asked.*** *`planPurchasedId` on Person exists to hold the **TrainingPeaks marketplace plan id** (the same ids in `data/training_plans_inventory.csv`). Nothing writes it today.* **The idea: create a Person on every plan sale** *— name, last name, email* ***when the buyer leaves one***, *plus the plan id and the purchase date —* **which makes the nurture audience accrue automatically instead of waiting on a migration.** *Then "you bought an 8-week swimming plan" becomes a query rather than a list-building exercise, and the plan id resolves the plan's name and length from the inventory, so no name needs storing.*
  ⚠️ **The reason it is parked and not queued: the email is optional.** *TrainingPeaks does not always pass a buyer address, so this audience is a subset of unknown size —* ***measure what share of recent sales carry an email before designing anything on top of it.*** *Also note the standing rule this bumps against: customer names and emails never enter the repo. Twenty is not the repo, so a Person record is fine; an export of it is not.*

- [ ] **#15 · Canonical exercise library for the members area — every decision made, nothing built.** *(Opened and fully specified September 8, 2026; **parked the same day by Iván (D5) so race pages keep the WIP slot**.)* **Home doc: `exercise-library-decisions.md`**, which owns D1–D5 and the reasoning behind each. Drafted cues: `exercise-library-canonical-cues.md`. Inventory: `data/members_exercises.csv` + `data/members_exercises_unique.csv`, generated by `automation/extract-members-exercises.js`.
  **The problem, measured rather than asserted:** the nine exercise artifacts hold **253 exercises in Spanish and the same in each of EN and PT** (parity verified, nothing missing) — **759 name+cue copies for 194 real exercises** *(derived September 8, 2026 by `automation/apply-exercise-merge-map.js`; re-run it rather than quoting this)*. *Editing one cue correctly today means editing up to 18 places across 15 files, which is why `core/` ended up calling cat-cow "Gato-vaca" while five other artifacts called it "Gato-camello".*
  **Design, settled:** `site/_data/exercises.json` — **same pattern and same precedent as `site/_data/library.json`**, whose own header records the identical failure on the marketing side. The library owns identity, words and media (`name`, `cue`, `tag`, `video`, three languages); **the artifact owns only the dose** (`sets`, `reps`, `rest`, `secs`) plus an optional one-line `note`. ⚠️ **A page may never override `cue`** — if a placement needs different words for the movement itself, it is a different exercise with its own id. *That single rule is what makes the library hold.*
  **Translations are carried, never regenerated** — the EN/PT text moves across from whichever artifact's cue wins. *They were confirmed correct on Aug 21; re-translating would quietly discard reviewed work.*
  **Verification is the gate, not a nicety:** build `_site` before and after, diff the rendered exercise text page by page, and **every difference must be a rename on the signed-off list.** Pattern exists in `tests/mobility-matrix.js`.
  ***What is already usable while this is parked:*** **the dedup decisions do not expire.** *The filming list is* **193 clips** *— 194 canonical exercises, of which bird dog's two entries share one. 33 of them appear in more than one artifact and cover 86 placements; Gato-camello alone covers six.* **One clip serves every placement.** ⚠️ *But the strength engine still has no `video` field at all (`activation-tool.js` has one and renders it; `strength-tool.js` does not), so **55 of the 194 canonical exercises have nowhere to put a video until that branch runs** — aquiles, rodillas, hombro, core-ciclista, core-corredor.*
  🔑 ***Scope addition, Iván's question September 8, 2026, and it is the half that makes the rest durable: the library must be consulted when the NEXT artifact is authored, or artifact #10 recreates the drift this session just spent a day deciding away.*** **`artifact-publish-runbook.md` says nothing about checking whether an exercise already exists** — it covers the engine, the template, the port and the verify, and a new tool's exercises are written from scratch every time. *That is exactly how `core/` came to call cat-cow "Gato-vaca" while five other artifacts called it "Gato-camello".* **What is needed is a lookup step that matches on the MOVEMENT, not the string** — an author writing "Gato-vaca", "Cat-cow" or "Gato camello" must be shown the existing `catcow` entry and reuse its id. *The near-duplicate pass built this session (`automation/cluster-exercise-duplicates.js`) already does that matching; the missing piece is running it against a candidate rather than against the corpus, and a runbook step that requires it.* ⚠️ **Build this in the same branch, not after** — a library nothing is obliged to consult is a suggestion.
  ✅ ***The name half already SHIPPED, September 8, 2026, ahead of this branch — 69 renames in three rounds across all three languages, all 33 clusters now reading one name everywhere*** *(`exercise-name-harmonisation.md`; regression check `automation/exercise-name-audit.js`).* **This branch inherits those names and must not re-decide them.** *It could ship early because names are display strings that already existed in all three languages — no new copy, no translation. The cues cannot: merged wording has to be written in Spanish and translated twice, which is precisely what makes it a branch.* ⚠️ *One cue edit rode along and is deliberate: renaming two pages to "vientre del gemelo" left them promising a restriction only aquiles explained, so D3a's guard sentence was lifted verbatim from aquiles into rodillas and movilidad in all three languages.*
  🚨 ***The SPANISH CUES also shipped, September 8, 2026 — Iván wrote all 33 harmonised cues and they are live across 86 placements in the nine Spanish pages*** *(`data/cue_harmonisation_signed.json`; `exercise-library-decisions.md` D6a–D6c).* ✅ ***EN and PT SHIPPED the same day — 172 cues and 89 names across both languages, Iván's text*** *(`data/cue_harmonisation_en_pt.json`, D7a–D7c).* **All three languages are harmonised; the debt opened in the morning was closed by evening.** ✅ ***And the remaining 166 cues followed the same day — the ENTIRE library, 253 placements × 3 languages, now carries Iván's harmonised names and cues*** *(`data/cue_remaining_signed.csv`, D8a–D8d).* **188 canonical exercises, 187 clips to film.** ⚠️ *Three content losses rode in with that pass — the breathing-tool link, the frog's recovery-day rule, and half the IT-band correction — **all caught by `tests/mobility-matrix.js` and `tests/recovery-matrix.js`, not by reading**, and restored from git. The test suite paid for itself in one session.* 🚩 ***One thing it exposed and did not fix: Portuguese has no register check.*** *Three PT cues arrived in* tu *where the other thirty use* você, *and they were caught by reading. Spanish has `automation/register-sweep.py` for exactly this — **a PT arm is worth building before the next Portuguese content pass.*** ~~⚠️ **EN and PT still carry the OLD cues. - [ ] **#11 · The TSS / CTL / ATL / TSB explainer — one proper long-form video plus written definitions.** *(Iván, Aug 12, 2026: "something I am always repeating and I have to do it properly.")* **This is the clearest "costs his time forever" item on the list**, which is the test this project applies to everything: it is explained by hand, repeatedly, to nearly every athlete and to leads mid-objection.
   **It already exists as a one-off and is doing real work.** A Loom recorded for a different athlete (`https://www.loom.com/share/22d14e5f153e4aa38178ab0ebe1f0765`) was sent to Roberto Fernández in the middle of the hardest question set any lead in the corpus asked, and it was part of closing him. **Do not re-record from nothing — that Loom is the outline.**
   **Two deliverables, deliberately, because they do different jobs.** The **video** carries the explanation an athlete watches once in week 1 and a lead watches while deciding. The **written definitions** are what someone re-reads in month four when they've forgotten what TSB means, and they are the half that is skimmable, linkable from a coaching message, and indexable — a glossary page is exactly the kind of page that ranks.
   **What it has to cover, from what actually gets asked:** what TSS is and why a number can be impossible (Nadine's run logged **1487 TSS** off a GPS elevation error — Iván caught and corrected it by hand); CTL/ATL/TSB in plain language; how to read the PMC chart; what "form" should look like going into a race; and **why the numbers can sit inside normal range and still be the wrong week to push** — that last one is the whole point of the weekly feedback loop and is the hardest thing to explain in a chat message.
   **Home:** the `/recursos/` hub in NOW, which currently has no page and no content — this is its first real content. Also reusable in the members area, in pre-sale (`sales-playbook.md` B4/B8), and in onboarding week 1.
   **The language trade-off, stated because it will otherwise be discovered late: a video is the one asset that does not translate cheaply.** That argues for the written definitions carrying the ES/EN/PT load and the video being Spanish-only to start, rather than delaying the whole thing until it can ship in three languages.
   Trigger: any time — it is writing and recording, not build work, so it does not queue behind the branch.

- [ ] **#17 · Mirror the rewritten CoachMatch ES sequence into `automation/`.** *(Opened September 9, 2026. Trigger: Iván imports the new copy into n8n.)* **The three Spanish emails were rewritten this session** — email 1 no longer carries the price, all three ask a question, and the day-5 break-up offers only the zones guide. *The diagnosis behind it: the n8n email always beat Iván's WhatsApp, so a lead read $149 with no discovery and no framing, then went silent — and the CRM recorded that as "no response". **Price losses were being absorbed by the no-response bucket**, which is why close #1's "price = 6.6% of losses" is a floor, not an estimate.* **Register verified**: `register-sweep.py --wide` returns 0 lines would change; the only flags are `escribí` and `bajé`, both first-person preterite. ⚠️ **Decisions taken with it, so they are not re-opened:** *(a)* **price stays at $149** — Iván has already told his athletes referrals and new customers are at that number; *(b)* **the unsubscribe footer was removed from the ES emails** rather than wired, because `unsubscribe_url` is produced nowhere in either workflow and was rendering an empty `href` (see the runbook correction below); *(c)* **PT stays as-is** — old copy, price still in PT email 1, no footer — *deliberate, not drift: the PT sequence sells All-Access as the language answer and is a different offer*; *(d)* **no reply alert** — replies to the new "respóndeme aquí" CTA land in the IMAP inbox with no notification, and Iván has accepted that.
  🔑 ***What this buys, and it is the reason to do it:*** *with the price out of the emails, **"no response" and "not interested — price" stop overlapping.** Labelled properly from here, six weeks of leads give the first honest read on whether $149 is the problem — which is the read the next pricing decision needs.*

- [ ] **Content engine — corpus awareness and a wider topic vocabulary.** *(Opened and parked Aug 30, 2026. Trigger was the GSC/GA4 pipeline.)* **These are one job.** The first stops the agent repeating what exists; the second gives it somewhere new to go. Doing only the first would leave it deduplicating its way into an ever-smaller space.
  ✅ ***CORPUS HALF DONE, COMMITTED AND VERIFIED IN THE RUN PATH — September 2–3, 2026.*** *Confirmed by reading the code rather than the note: `published_corpus()`, `corpus_idf()` and `coverage_block()` are called at the top of the run, and `screen_ideas()` is called on the generated ideas — it drops near-duplicates with a printed reason and annotates the survivors. Not a prompt rule; computed.* **What remains under this bullet is only the second half — a wider topic vocabulary. `writer_agent.py` still enforces a closed nine-slug list** *(`running, cycling, swimming, triathlon, nutrition, recovery, physiology, strength, weight-loss`)*, ***which is the constraint the original note warned about: deduplicating inside a fixed vocabulary narrows the space it can write in.*** *Not urgent — the blog has never been clicked, so "somewhere new to go" is a distribution question before it is a vocabulary one.*
  ~~***HALF DONE — the corpus half was built September 2, 2026, unparked early by Iván after the Sep 2 batch made it seven duplicate pairs rather than three.***~~ *The trigger was wrong: it assumed GSC was needed, and it was not. Waiting for it cost two more batches.*
  **What shipped, in `research_agent.py`:** `published_corpus()` reads every live article from the `.njk` files (the files, not `content_pieces` — they are what the site serves, they include the six hand-written articles with no database row, and they exclude rows published then removed). `corpus_idf()` + `dup_score()` score a proposed title against the archive; `screen_ideas()` **drops** clones at ≥0.45, **annotates** anything ≥0.28 with its two nearest published articles written into `rationale`, which is the field the review page already renders, and also compares ideas **against each other within one batch**. `coverage_block()` hands the agent article counts per topic and language.
  ***The root cause was not "it cannot see what is published" — it could.*** *The prompt has always carried a list headed "ALREADY PROPOSED OR PUBLISHED (do not repeat)". It was the wrong list — `SELECT working_title FROM content_ideas`, so idea working titles rather than published headlines, rejected ideas mixed in with live ones, and no language, topic or slug to compute anything from — and, more importantly, **nothing checked it.** The agent proposed a second "El método noruego simplificado" with the first one's title in its own context window.* **This is the convergence lesson again**, which went from 2/12 to 8/12 usable ideas the day it moved out of the prompt and into `theme_clusters()`: *a model asked to check something against a long list will say it did; code that computes it does.*
  **Verified by replaying the failures**: all seven duplicate pairs are caught, three of them dropped outright, the rest flagged with the true twin named; and four genuinely new ideas (open-water goggles, GPS altimetry, carbon plates, a marathon world record) pass clean. Both suites in the session log.
  ***Still open: the vocabulary half below.*** *And one honest limitation — title similarity is a proxy. It caught every duplicate we know about, but it cannot see two articles that answer the same question in unrelated words. Traction data is what would.*

  **Teach the research agent what has already been written — the highest-value change left in the content engine now the plumbing works.** *(Opened Aug 30, 2026, from evidence in that day's batch. **BUILT Sep 2, 2026** — see the note above.)*
  **The evidence, not a worry — three near-duplicates in one batch of 20:**
  1. **`El método noruego simplificado`** (ES, physiology) — Aug 30 shares a *literal headline prefix* with the Aug 4 article. Three weeks apart, same subject.
  2. **Threshold intensity control** (ES, physiology) — *"Control de Intensidad en Series de Umbral"* and *"Regulación de intensidad: cómo ajustar tus ritmos de umbral en tiempo real"*, **both written the same day**. The agent does not even deduplicate within its own batch.
  3. **Marathon durability** (PT, running) — Aug 30 *"Durabilidade Muscular na Maratona"* against Aug 4 *"Durabilidade na Maratona: Como evitar o muro"*.
  *Deliberately NOT on this list: the marathon volume/plan cluster and the weight-loss trio, which the same scan flagged. Those are one subject from genuinely different angles — that is what topical authority looks like, and pruning it would be the wrong lesson.*
  **Two inputs, and the cheap one is not the interesting one.** (a) The published corpus — headline, topic, `trans_key`, date — so it stops re-proposing what exists. Needs no GSC. (b) Traction from GSC — the position 5–20 near-misses — so it proposes *improving* an article that is almost competitive rather than writing a second one next to it. **(b) is what turns deduplication into a strategy:** the right response to "we already covered this" is usually to strengthen the existing article, not to skip the topic.
  **Also fix within-batch duplication** — case 2 above is a single generation call proposing two versions of one idea, which no amount of corpus history would catch.

  **The topic vocabulary is quietly acting as a content strategy, and it is too small to sustain the publishing rate.** *(Iván, Aug 30, 2026 — the sharper version of "no swimming articles", and it reframes the corpus-awareness item above.)*
  **The observation:** nine topics, all evergreen training themes. There are only so many articles to write about threshold training; once five exist the sixth overlaps, which is exactly what the Aug 30 batch produced. **Evergreen topics saturate. Timely ones do not** — new equipment, races, results and records happen continuously. At ~20 articles a batch the finite space gets consumed fast.
  **Why the agent cannot currently propose them, which is not "it did not think of it":**
  1. **The `our_assets` rule filters them out.** Every idea must name concrete Triaperformance assets or be discarded as "one anyone could write." A sound quality filter that doubles as a **topic** filter: new tech, race news and world records have no matching plan, methodology section or members artifact, so they are excluded before anything else is weighed. *This is the change that matters — adding topics without relaxing this changes nothing.*
  2. **The agent has never been told the topic vocabulary exists.** `topic` was added to the writer only, so ideas are not topic-tagged and coverage cannot be reasoned about at all.
  3. `gear` **already exists as an `article_type`** and has produced zero articles, because it was gated on the affiliate program (NEXT #9), which is itself gated on traffic. A designed-in capability that has been switched off by a dependency chain.
  **What to add, with the trade-offs stated rather than assumed:**
  - **`gear` / tech as a topic** — directly serves the affiliate plan, and the article type already exists. Strongest candidate.
  - **`racing`** — races, previews, results. **Check against NEXT #1 first**: the race-landing-pages initiative already owns race content, with 12 races researched in `data/races.csv`. Two initiatives covering races would be the same duplication problem one level up.
  - **News and world records** — *the honest counterpoint*: short traffic life, strong on Instagram, weak for SEO. The EN/PT strategy is explicitly SEO and affiliate, so news serves ES/social and not the other two markets. Worth having, worth not measuring like the rest.
  - **`swimming`** — 36 groups, zero articles, in a business whose core market is triathlon. Ten photos already sit behind an empty pill.
  **Feed the agent coverage counts per topic**, not just the list, so "we have 19 running and 0 swimming" is an input rather than something nobody sees.

For the August cohort that is roughly **November 2026**. No amount of backfill moves this, because the pages did not exist.*

The data got older; the articles did not.*** *The Aug 12 note's original sequencing — distribution before the feedback loop, because measurement with no traffic measures nothing — was right, and this claim briefly overturned it on a misreading.* **Read the `word_count` note in §9 before writing the agent** — *one of the first five rows returned was an LLM prompt, not a query.*

Do not scope or build the pipeline from the text below: it is the Aug 12 record, and* ***two of its statements did not survive verification*** *— see the item in NOW and `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` §9. Kept in full because the reasoning is the record.*
  **What is still genuinely LATER is the agent itself**, and its trigger is unchanged: enough GSC history to rank against. *Note the backfill may satisfy that on day one rather than in three months — so the honest gate on starting the agent is the pipeline being* ***verified***, *not the calendar.*
  ***Instrumentation scoped Aug 12, 2026 — SUPERSEDED, retained as the record. The blocker is not the agent, it is that no performance data reaches the VPS at all.*** *The research agent proposes topics with zero knowledge of which past articles earned impressions, rankings or plan clicks — an open-loop content factory. Three things came out of scoping it:*
  **An MCP connector is the wrong mechanism, and it's worth being precise about why.** The registry has no first-party Google connector (only Supermetrics, a paid aggregator). But even a perfect one would only let *Claude in a conversation* query the data. **The agents run on cron with no Claude in the loop** — for the feedback to reach them, the data has to land in Postgres where a script can read it. Connector ≠ agent access.
  **The build is a copy of something already running.** `automation/analytics/sync_pixel_data.py` (05:00 cron) already authenticates a Google service account, pulls BigQuery, writes Postgres. ~~GSC's Search Analytics API and the GA4 Data API are the same shape with different endpoints.~~ *Corrected Aug 29, 2026 — half right. GSC did resolve to the Search Analytics API. **GA4 did not**: its BigQuery export was already running into the same project the pixel script reads, so the GA4 half needed no new endpoint, no new credential and no new grant at all — and the Data API would have been actively worse, because it silently suppresses rows at low user counts, which is exactly this site's regime.* Iván's part: enable both APIs in the Cloud project, add the service account under Search Console → Users and permissions and GA4 → Property Access Management (Viewer). Possibly the same `pixel-sync-vps` account, keeping it to one credential.
  ~~**Both APIs expose ~16 months of history**~~ *Corrected Aug 29, 2026 — the 16 months is a Search Console fact, not a shared one. **GA4 has no history to backfill**: the property and its BigQuery export were created the same day (July 21, 2026), so the export already covers its entire life. The 16-month claim survives where it matters — GSC — and it is the whole reason the GSC half uses the API rather than the bulk export, which is verifiably forward-only.* So this backfills rather than only accruing forward — the "60–90 days" trigger above may already be satisfied for the pre-existing pages the day it's built.
  **The signal that matters is near-misses, not traffic.** The highest-value input for the research agent is the set of queries where the site already ranks position 5–20 with real impressions: demand Google has confirmed, on topics already almost competitive. *"Write a better article about the thing you're on page two for"* is a far stronger instruction than *"here's what four blogs wrote about last week."*
  *Expectation note, so this isn't judged too early: 13 articles, 10 published in the last 8 days. Zero reads at that age is the calendar, not the content. **The thing that would be a real problem is if the pages aren't indexed** — checkable today in Search Console → Indexing → Pages, no build required. Do that before building any pipeline.*

- [ ] **Content engine V2 — reducing the two review gates.** *(Scoped Aug 12, 2026. Not queued: the honest sequence puts distribution and the feedback loop first, since a review gate should only be loosened once there's evidence it's rubber-stamping.)*
  **The principle: don't remove a gate on a hunch — and the evidence already exists, unread.** `content_pieces` stores `original_body` alongside `body` specifically so the model's output can be diffed against what Iván approved. **Nobody has ever looked at it.** A near-zero edit rate across twenty `plan_guide` pieces is an argument for loosening; anything else isn't. That diff is the prerequisite for everything below.
  **Translation approval should go first, and it's the one that can go honestly.** You can't automate *"is this true?"* — but you can automate *"does this say the same thing as the article already approved?"* **Translation is the only stage with a ground truth to compare against.** A fidelity check could verify mechanically that the sibling names the same entities, carries identical physiological numbers, links plans of the same sport/distance, and matches section structure; flag only semantic drift, auto-approve the rest. That removes a third of the approvals, and the Valencia→CIM swap is exactly what it would have caught — the entity changed while everything around it stayed put.
  **Article approval shouldn't be removed, only made cheaper.** Today it means reading 1,400 words cold. A pre-flight report — every numeric claim extracted with whether it traces to `methodology.md`, plan cards listed with their metric/duration spread, assertions appearing nowhere in the KB — turns reading into checking. A second model as adversarial reviewer (annotating, not approving) would plausibly have flagged the 120–160 g/h carbohydrate figure.
  **The idea queue should shrink, not the gate.** *(Iván's observation, Aug 12.)* The research agent predates the translator and still proposes EN and PT ideas for universal topics the translator now covers free. Approving an EN idea *and* letting an ES article translate produces **two English articles competing on the same topic**. EN/PT ideas are only worth approving when genuinely market-specific — a US race, a Portuguese-catalogue constraint. Restricting generation accordingly would cut the queue meaningfully.

Do not read that as "GBP is a channel now": what is confirmed is API-family access, not the local-post endpoint. See the verification item in NOW before scoping GBP into this build.***

## Snapshot — September 11, 2026: `kb-hygiene-prompt.md` before it became a checklist

### Weekly KB Hygiene — the prompt

*Written August 8, 2026, distilled from the consolidation session that produced `open-loops-archive.md` and the "one home per initiative" rule. Everything outside the fenced block is context for why the pass is shaped this way — the prompt itself is the fenced block.*

**This runs on a schedule now — Tuesdays, 08:00 America/Cordoba, from September 8, 2026.** *A scheduled task opens a fresh session bound to Iván's computer, reads this file, and follows the fenced block. It does not need pasting. Editing the block below changes what the next run does; that is the point of keeping the instructions in the repo rather than in the task.*

⚠️ **Two things a scheduled run cannot do, and the prompt is written to handle both rather than stall on them:** *it cannot reach the VPS, TrainingPeaks, n8n or Twenty — those come back as an explicit command block for Iván — and it must never commit or push.* *If a run reports nothing worth changing, that is a real result. A pass that always finds something is a pass that is inventing findings.*

**Cadence:** weekly, ~30–45 min, Tuesday morning. *Deliberately not a build day — the output is a decision list and it competes with itself if a branch is mid-flight.*

---

#### The prompt

```
Act as my knowledge-base editor for Triaperformance. Prompt me for folder access
to the local repo first.

This is a weekly hygiene pass, not a build session. No feature work. Read before
you write, report before you edit, and never commit or push.

=== STEP 0: IS THE MAP THE MAP? (30 seconds, do it first) ===

Compare the project instructions THIS session received against the repo mirror
triaperformance-project-instructions.md. List any doc named in the mirror's KB
index that is missing from what actually loaded, and tell me to repaste.

A session cannot grep for a document it has not been told exists, and since
Aug 12, 2026 the index IS the map -- the project knowledge is deliberately empty.
Added Sep 2, 2026: the mirror had 47 index entries, the loaded copy had 38.
Fourteen docs were invisible, including monthly-close-runbook.md and
tenure-analysis.md. The mirror had been edited as recently as Aug 30; the field
was simply never repasted.

Then diff every key in site/_data/library.json against the filesystem --
find site/members -ipath '*<slug>*' -- for all three language blocks, every key.
Language versions are SUBDIRECTORIES, so listing site/members/ tells you nothing
about what is inside en/ and pt/. Two passes have now reported a tool as missing
or present on a check that could not have seen it.

=== STEP 1: VERIFY LIVE STATE AGAINST LIVE SYSTEMS ===

Before anything else, print the first 5 lines of every SETUP.md, *-runbook.md and
*-brief.md, and flag any "Status:" / "not deployed" / "not built" claim in them.
Added Aug 13, 2026: one such line sat wrong at the top of a runbook for two and a
half weeks, saying an engine was unbuilt while it ran on cron. It survived
because normal work never opens those files. This costs 30 seconds.


Do this FIRST, before reading any doc's claims as true. The single most expensive

- Fetch triaperformance.com/planes/, /en/plans/, /pt/planos/. Note what each
  prints as its plan count.

    * prices -> triaperformance-pricing-and-positioning.md

  THE TEST, before reporting any figure as a finding: who acts differently once

=== STEP 2: THE COMPLETE OPEN-ITEMS LIST ===

🚨 BEFORE LISTING ANYTHING AS OPEN, CHECK WHETHER IT IS ALREADY DONE.

executed days earlier and were still being reported back to Iván as open: the
All-Access `Insert Token in Postgres` check, the Private 1:1 checkout URL --
which triaperformance-pricing-and-positioning.md had carried since the previous
day -- and the name backfill, which ran and found nothing to change.)

The test is NOT whether the checkbox is empty. It is whether the doc or the
system that OWNS the thing still agrees it is open. For every item whose action
is Iván's -- a paste into n8n, a field in Twenty, a URL into a doc, a script on
the VPS -- grep the owning doc for the thing it asks for before you report it.

Sept 2's pass concluded that ticking a box is not closing an item. This is the
same failure one step earlier: work done in the world and never ticked at all.
Reporting done work back as open is how a list stops being read.


- Read open-loops.md. That's the live list.
- Then find every open item that is NOT in it. Grep the whole repo for unchecked
  boxes, "still open", "not yet", "pending", "TODO", "open question", "to verify",

- Give me ONE consolidated list: what's in open-loops.md, and separately what was

- Flag anything in open-loops.md that I've already told you is done in a past

- Check the WIP rule honestly: 1 big branch + 1 small slot. If the small slot is
  over its limit, say the number out loud. Don't quietly accept it.

=== STEP 3: INCONSISTENCIES ===

- Any figure stated differently in two docs. Name the owner doc and every copy.
- Any doc calling something open/pending that open-loops.md shows as closed.
- Any present-tense claim that's no longer true (things "being built" that are
  built, things "that don't exist yet" that do, tools described as living
  somewhere they no longer live).
- Any retracted claim still circulating — INCLUDING claims restated inside a
  correction note in order to deny them. A denial keeps the claim alive; the next
  reader quotes the denial. Check your own past correction notes for this.
- Any customer-facing copy in the repo (GBP posts, listing rewrites, page copy)
  carrying a stale number. These are the expensive ones — they're published.

=== STEP 4: DUPLICATION AND RETIREMENT ===

- Measure it, don't eyeball it: script a near-duplicate passage count between
  every pair of docs (normalise to word sets, Jaccard > ~0.55, sentences > 90
  chars). Report the top pairs with counts.
- For each hot pair: name which doc should OWN that content, and propose the
  other side be cut to a pointer.
- Apply the standing rule: one home per initiative, one home per list. A doc that
  has finished its job gets RETIRED — its live pieces moved into the owning doc,
  then deleted — not left in the index to be re-read and re-quoted as current.
  Name any doc that now qualifies.
- Report total repo word count vs. last week's, so the trend is visible.

=== STEP 5: REPORT, THEN ASK ===

Give me the findings first. Then split proposed fixes into:
  (a) mechanical and clearly right — stale numbers, closed items, dead
      references. Propose doing these immediately.
  (b) structural — deleting or merging docs, moving sections, changing what a
      doc owns. Wait for my go-ahead on each.

Push back on me where the data disagrees with what I've said. Lead with the
answer, keep it short, cut any word that doesn't change the meaning.

=== HOUSE RULES WHILE EDITING ===

- Corrections go in as dated inline notes that say what the line used to claim
  and why it was wrong. Append, don't silently rewrite.
- EXCEPTION: a claim that was retracted as false gets DELETED, not struck
  through and not restated in its own correction note. Struck-through text still
  reads as text.
- Never rewrite dated build records in ai-infrastructure-documentation.md to
  current values. They were correct on their date. Append a note at the end of
  the section saying the figures are historical and naming the owner file.
- Don't create a new doc to solve doc bloat unless it's an archive split.
- Never add, commit or push. Tell me what to commit.

=== END OF SESSION ===

- Update open-loops.md: live items only.
- Move anything closed to open-loops-archive.md with its closing note intact —
  the reasoning is what stops the question being re-asked in six weeks.
- Add one session-log entry to open-loops-archive.md.
- If a doc was added, renamed or retired, update the KB index in
  triaperformance-project-instructions.md in the same session.
- REMIND ME TO PASTE triaperformance-project-instructions.md INTO THE PROJECT'S
  CUSTOM INSTRUCTIONS FIELD. Unconditionally, every pass -- not only on weeks
  when a doc changed. Added Sep 2, 2026: the conditional version let the field
  fall fourteen docs behind the mirror across sessions where nothing changed.
- Tell me explicitly whether anything this session met the bar for updating
  project Memory. Most weeks nothing will, and that's the correct answer.
```

#### The status-claim audit — a separate, one-off pass

*Added August 13, 2026, after `automation/content-engine/SETUP.md` was found opening with **"Status: written, nothing deployed"** for two and a half weeks while both agents, the writer and the admin page had been live since Aug 4. Same day, `triaperformance-business-overview.md` still said "nine tools are live" against a library that had been rebuilt, and all three All-Access pages sold a members area containing a tool deleted that morning.*

**Why the weekly pass did not catch it, which is the whole point.** Step 1 of the weekly prompt already says "verify live state against live systems" — and this still happened, three times, in one repo, in one day. Two reasons:

1. **The weekly pass reads the docs it reads.** `open-loops.md`, the strategy docs, the infra doc. It does not open `automation/*/SETUP.md`, because the index explicitly says runbooks are "not read during normal work". *A stale line in a file nobody opens is invisible to a pass that only checks files it opens.*
2. **The damage is asymmetric and the weekly pass treats it symmetrically.** A doc claiming something is live when it isn't causes a confused five minutes. ***A doc claiming something is NOT built when it is causes weeks of nothing*** — anyone deciding whether to build on the content engine read line 3 and stopped. That asymmetry deserves its own sweep.

**Run this once, mechanically, across the whole repo. It is not the weekly pass and should not be folded into it** — the weekly pass is a judgement session with a WIP limit; this is a grep-driven inventory that either finds contradictions or doesn't.

```
Act as my knowledge-base auditor for Triaperformance. Prompt me for folder access
to the local repo first.

ONE JOB: find every place a document asserts BUILD OR DEPLOY STATE, and check
whether that assertion is still true. Nothing else. No feature work, no
restructuring, no new docs. Never commit or push.

Background: on Aug 13, 2026 automation/content-engine/SETUP.md opened with
"Status: written, nothing deployed" while the whole engine had been live on cron
for two and a half weeks. Nobody noticed because it is the first line of a file
that normal work never opens. The same day, two other docs and three sales pages
were found describing things that had changed.

=== STEP 1: SWEEP, DON'T READ ===

Grep the entire repo -- including automation/**, site/**, every runbook, and
every *.md -- for claims about state. Search at minimum:

  not deployed, nothing deployed, not built, not yet, no todavía, todavía no,
  pending, still pending, planned, upcoming, próximamente, coming soon, TODO,
  WIP, "written but", "designed but", "in progress", "no live", "not live",
  "Status:", "Estado:", "Build status", "not wired", "unbuilt", "parked"

Also list, separately: THE FIRST 5 LINES OF EVERY FILE named SETUP.md, *-runbook.md,
or *-brief.md. Status headers live at the top of files, and the top of a file
is what a future reader treats as current.

Output a table before changing anything: file, line, the claim, and which
system would confirm or refute it.

=== STEP 2: VERIFY AGAINST A DIFFERENT SOURCE THAN THE CLAIM ===

For each row, check against something OTHER than the doc that made the claim:

  - Code/pages exist?        -> the filesystem, and the built output in _site/
  - Deployed on the VPS?     -> ASK ME. Give me the exact command to run
                                (docker ps, crontab -l, curl a health endpoint).
                                You have no VPS access; do not infer from the repo.
  - Live on the website?     -> fetch the real URL
  - n8n / Twenty / TP?       -> ASK ME. Never infer from the reference JSON;
                                that file is documentation, not the system.

If you cannot verify a row, say so and leave it. An unverified row stays in the
report as unverified; it does not get "corrected" on a guess.

=== STEP 3: SORT BY BLAST RADIUS, NOT BY COUNT ===

Report in this order, and say plainly which is which:

  A. SAYS NOT BUILT / NOT DEPLOYED, BUT IT IS.  <- most expensive, fix first.
     These silently stop work. Name what each one has been blocking.
  B. SAYS LIVE / DONE, BUT IT ISN'T.            <- causes wrong decisions.
  C. Numbers that moved (counts, prices, subscriber and athlete figures).
  D. Named things that no longer exist (retired tools, deleted pages, renamed
     products) still being described as current -- especially in anything
     customer-facing.

=== STEP 4: FIX, THE REPO'S WAY ===

  - Corrections are DATED INLINE NOTES that say what the line used to claim and
    why it was wrong. Append; do not silently rewrite. The wrong version is the
    evidence.
  - Grep for the CLAIM after fixing, not for the correction -- a correction that
    quotes the stale claim keeps it findable and quotable.
  - One home per figure. If a number appears in more than one file, fix every
    copy in this same pass and say which file owns it.

=== STEP 5: THE STRUCTURAL QUESTION ===

For each status line found, ask whether it should exist at all. A runbook
describes a PROCEDURE; build state belongs in open-loops.md, which is read at the
start of every session. A "Status:" header on a runbook is a copy of open-loops
that nobody updates.

Propose -- do not apply -- either:
  (a) delete the status line and let open-loops.md own it, or
  (b) keep it and add "verified: <date>", so the next reader can see its age.

=== END ===

  - Give me a commit message and the list of files changed. I commit and push.
  - Add any NEW failure mode you found to kb-hygiene-prompt.md, so the weekly
    pass inherits it.
  - Tell me if anything met the bar for project Memory. Usually nothing does.
```

##### Failure modes found on the first real run of this pass (August 14, 2026) — fold these into the weekly Step 1

*The audit above was run once, mechanically, on Aug 14, 2026. It found nine patterns the weekly pass would not have caught, plus a tenth about the auditing itself (item 10, resolved Aug 17). Each is now a one-line check.*

1. **A status claim inside a HEADING is the least-revised line in any file.** `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` §15 was titled *"(built July 26, 2026; not yet cut over)"* for **sixteen days** after the cutover, in the technical source of truth, where it also renders in the table of contents. Nobody edits a heading when appending a dated note underneath it. **Check: grep `^#{1,4} .*(not |un|pending|TBD|WIP)` across all `*.md`.**
2. **A "trigger to build: X ships" line is a status claim that nothing re-reads on the day X ships.** `race-landing-pages-longlist.md` and `race-page-content-outline.md` both said *"not blocking anything — trigger is the plan template shipping."* The plan template shipped **Aug 6**; `open-loops.md` had already promoted race pages to NEXT #1; both headers still read as parked eight days later. **Check: for every "trigger:" in the repo, ask whether the trigger has already fired.** A parked doc never notices its own unparking.
3. **The correction landed in the file nobody opens and missed the file everybody opens.** On Aug 13 `automation/content-engine/SETUP.md` was fixed — correctly. `content-engine-brief.md`, the doc the KB index calls *"the status-driven content pipeline design"*, still said **"Still not built: the `content` Postgres database and the five agents"** and **"Status: design"**. **Check: when you correct a status line, grep the repo for the *system's name* and fix every sibling claim in the same session.** One fix is never one file.
4. **A "what's left to build" table is the most expensive thing in the repo to leave stale, because its whole purpose is to be trusted.** `artifact-publish-runbook.md`'s "In progress" table — which `open-loops.md` points at *by name* — listed the zone calculator as **"not started"** four days after it shipped in three languages, and the activation artifact as **"awaiting port"** the day after it shipped. **Check: any table whose column is called State, Status or Shipped gets re-derived from the filesystem, not read.**
5. **A deleted page leaves five copies of its URL behind, and one of them is a procedure.** Aug 13's retirements (`zonas`, `tests`, `carrera`, `kettlebell`, `nutricion`, `activacion-ciclismo`) were still listed as current in `website-build-cutover-runbook.md`'s verification checklist (which sends the operator to five 404s), `automation/content-engine/SETUP.md`, `triaperformance-growth-roadmap.md` twice, and `artifact-publish-runbook.md`. **Check: on the day a page is deleted, grep its path across `*.md`, `*.njk`, `*.json` and `automation/` — not just `site/`.**
6. **A `_note` inside a data file claiming the file is incomplete.** `site/_data/zonesUi.json` opened with *"Spanish only for now"* while holding fully populated `es`, `en` and `pt` objects. **Check: grep `_note`/`_comment` keys in `site/_data/*.json` against what the file actually contains.**

7. **A stale open-items list does not merely fail to inform — it manufactures work.** Two entries on `infrastructure.html`'s list (the Managed Hermes cancellation, the Twenty API-key rotation) were closed long ago, and the list caused them to be re-asked of Iván **three separate times**. *Each re-ask spends trust in the list, and a list nobody trusts is a list nobody updates.* **Check: any list of open items outside `open-loops.md` is a defect, not a convenience.** That one was deleted Aug 14, 2026.
8. **A status claim written in the FUTURE tense never expires on its own.** `zones-calculator-brief.md` carried *"They promise the calculator is behind the login. **It is about to be public.**"* True for one day; it then read as a pending future event indefinitely. Likewise `plan-capture.js`'s *"NOT LIVE YET **as of this commit**"* — the code ships, the sentence stays, and nothing rebuilds it. **Check: grep `about to`, `as of this commit`, `will be`, `once X ships` — and never put deploy state in a source comment.**
9. **A runbook written the day before its system goes live is stale on arrival.** `zone-magnet-runbook.md` was created Aug 13 opening **"Status: NOT LIVE"** and was false within 24 hours — the shortest-lived stale claim in the repo. *This is not carelessness; it is structural. A deploy runbook is by definition written before the deploy.* **Since Aug 14, 2026 every `Status:` line on a runbook or brief carries a `verified: <date>` stamp.** Ten had none. **Check: a `Status:` line with no `verified:` date is itself a finding — stamp it or delete it.**

10. **The verification instrument has its own cache, and it fails in the direction that matters.** *(Found Aug 14, 2026; diagnosed correctly Aug 17.)* *`/en/plans/` served **108 plans**; the same URL with `?v=audit` appended served **111**, the correct number.* ***First diagnosed as a problem with the site's `Cache-Control` headers, and that was wrong — the correction is the useful part.*** *Iván's header check returned `cache-control: no-cache` with no `age`/`via`/`x-cache`, so Caddy was serving correctly all along; the stale copy lived in **Claude's own web-fetch layer**, which caches per URL. The tell was arithmetic, not headers: the three catalogs returned **ES 164 / EN 108 / PT 53**, a combination* ***no single deploy of the site has ever produced*** *(EN 108 last co-existed with PT 29; PT 53 only exists post-Aug 12). One cached page shows one moment; three independently-aged cache entries show three.*
  **The rule, unchanged and now better justified: always append a cache-busting query string when checking a live URL in a hygiene pass.** *Not because the CDN might be stale — because the fetch tool can hand back a cached page and thereby* ***confirm*** *a stale claim with what reads as independent evidence. This audit came one URL away from "verifying" a catalogue of 108 and dispatching someone to find three plans that were never missing.*
  ***And the part that generalises past caching:*** *the pass's own 🚨 top finding was a false alarm that blamed the user's infrastructure for the auditor's tooling. **"Verify against a different source than the one that made the claim" includes the tool making the check.** What settled it was a header read Claude cannot perform and a consistency argument over the repo's git history — two sources, neither of them the fetch. **When a live check disagrees with the repo, suspect the check before filing the finding.***

11. **A per-language site holds three copies of every hand-typed figure, and fixing the one you were shown is a third of the fix.** *(Found September 2, 2026.)* *On Aug 27 the ES homepage was caught hand-typing* **"45 reseñas"** *against an owner doc that had said* **46** *since Aug 14. It was fixed properly — `site/_data/site.json` gained `reviewCount`, `site/index.njk` was rewired to render it, and a `_reviewCountNote` was written asserting* **"This is the site's single rendering copy so no page hand-types it again."** ***That sentence was false when it was written.*** `site/en/index.njk` *and* `site/pt/index.njk` *each still hand-typed* **45**, *and kept doing so for another six days — nineteen days stale in total, longer than the ES page the pass was looking at.* **Check: when a hand-typed figure is found on any page, grep its ES, EN and PT siblings in the same breath — and treat a `_note` that claims single-sourcing as a hypothesis to test, not a fact to trust.** *The note is written by the same session that made the fix, so it records the intention, never the coverage.*

12. **A directory listing of the parent does not tell you whether the language siblings exist.** *(Found September 2, 2026, closing an item this same pass opened on Aug 24.)* *The Aug 24 pass reported* **"no EN/PT version of the tool exists anywhere in `site/members/`"** *and raised it 🚨 as live marketing copy selling a nonexistent product. `site/members/en/cyclist-core/` existed the whole time — the language versions are* ***subdirectories***, *not suffixes, so `ls site/members/` shows `en` and `pt` as two entries and reveals nothing about what is inside them.* **Check: diff every key in `site/_data/library.json` against the filesystem with `find site/members -ipath '*<slug>*'`, for every key in all three language blocks, every pass — not just the key someone asked about.** *Run properly, that check finds the one that is still live: `strength` is listed as `live`/`gated` in the EN and PT blocks with no `site/members/en/strength/` or `site/members/pt/forca/` behind it. **The Aug 24 pass had this in front of it and looked at one key.***

**What this pass is not.** Not a rewrite, not a consolidation, not a place to fix writing. If a doc is stale *and* badly organised, fix the staleness here and log the reorganisation for the weekly pass. *The reason this is separate from the weekly pass is that it is boring and mechanical, and boring mechanical passes are the only kind that reliably find this class of error.*

#### Why each step is shaped this way

**Step 1 runs first, and against live systems, because the repo's worst failure mode is documentary, not technical.** Four times now a state written down has outlived reality: the "6 dead plans" myth, the `plans_raw` duplicate ghost, a storefront documented as undeployed for a week while live (holding four queued items behind a gate that had already opened), and a race-stamped plan still serving on the live catalog after the sweep that was supposed to have caught it. That last one was found by reading the live page, not the CSV — the sweep had been verified against the same file that produced it, which can only ever confirm itself. **Verify against a different source than the one that made the claim.**

**Step 2 exists because `open-loops.md` is only the single source of truth if something actively enforces it.** On Aug 8 there were eleven genuinely open items living only in runbooks, `methodology.md` §13, and the infra doc — invisible to every session that started by reading the list. Items don't migrate on their own.

**Step 3's fourth bullet is the one that's easy to skip.** In this session the first correction pass replaced a retired claim with a note that quoted the claim while explaining its deletion — reproducing the exact mechanism that had kept it alive for three weeks. It was caught by grep, not by reading. Grep for the claim, not for the correction.

**Step 4 is measured rather than judged** because "these two docs feel similar" doesn't survive a disagreement and "12 near-duplicate passages between these two files" does. That measurement is what justified retiring the storefront brief.

**Step 5's split matters** because mechanical fixes are cheap and reversible, structural ones delete things. Bundling them means either the safe fixes wait on a decision, or the deletions happen without one.

#### What this pass is NOT for

Not a planning session, not a build session, and not a place to open a new branch. If it surfaces something that deserves real work, it goes into `open-loops.md` with a trigger and waits for a session with the WIP room to take it. A hygiene pass that turns into a build session stops being run weekly, and a hygiene pass that isn't run weekly is how the repo got here.

---

#### Step 11 — Expired plans, added August 30, 2026

**Grep for the future tense and check whether the future arrived.** Patterns worth searching across the repo:
`not yet built`, `not yet done`, `planned to become`, `could eventually`, `future `, `will be`, `next step`, `neither built`, `still to come`.

For each hit, apply the three-way sort now in the project instructions under *Append, don't rewrite*:
**a wrong belief is kept and struck; an expired plan is deleted; a moved figure is just fixed.**

***Why this step exists.*** *On Aug 30, 2026 the GSC/GA4 pipeline shipped, and four separate lines across `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` and `infrastructure.html` were still describing it in the future tense — "future GSC pulls", "GA4's export could eventually sync", "neither built yet". The first instinct was to annotate all four with pointers to the new addendum, which is what the append rule literally says. Iván stopped it:* ***that produces a to-do, an "unbuilt" note, a "built" note and a pointer, for one thing that simply exists now.*** *An expired plan is not a correction. It has no lesson in it, and every session after this one pays to read it.*

*This is the cheapest step in the pass and the one that shrinks the repo, which is the only mechanism by which these docs get smaller over time instead of larger.*

## Snapshot — September 11, 2026: the "Last updated" line of `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md`

*One line, 44058 bytes, carrying every session summary since August. The numbered sections of that doc hold the same history; this is kept only so nothing is lost.*

**Last updated:** September 11, 2026 (**§52 — the ideas agent had never been told a free public tool exists, and the assets block was cut mid-JSON.** *`public_tools` now derives from the Recursos menu, `lead_magnets` from an `ls`, and a new `tool` CTA type carries the free/ungated case.* 🚨 ***The pre-existing half is the worse one: `json.dumps(assets)[:6000]` against a ~13.5 KB payload had been handing the model a JSON document that does not close — `lead_magnets` never reached the prompt at all, and `races` arrived a third short.*** 🔑 **Slicing a serialised structure by character count both correct, one true. A `path_regexp` redirect in `automation/Caddyfile` now maps the whole `/es/` subtree onto its root equivalent (`/es/planes/` → `/planes/`), permanent. Regex rather than `path /es/*` because the subtree has to be preserved, and non-capturing so it cannot also swallow `/espanol` or `/estiramientos`. This also closes the `/es/` 404 that §16 has recorded as "expected" since July 29.) *Same-day earlier entry (**§9 addendum — GSC + GA4 into analytics Postgres, VERIFIED LIVE**: Search Console and GA4 now land in `analytics-postgres` nightly at 05:15 and 05:45, backfilled to **2025-05-06** — ~16 months, because the Domain property had been collecting through the HubSpot era off the legacy DNS record, so verification exposed the history rather than starting it. 🚨 **A claim that the content-engine feedback agent's "60–90 days" trigger was therefore satisfied was made and RETRACTED in the same session** — *the 16 months belong to the domain, most of it the HubSpot site; the blog only exists since Aug 4, 2026, and the first five near-miss rows contain zero articles. Article-level performance still needs ~November. GA4 via the BigQuery export already running, because the property and its export were created the same day so there was never any history to lose. **Four defects surfaced during handover and every one was found by running something rather than reading it** — a repo path that does not exist on the box (`~/triaperformance-docs`; it is `~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs`), an illegal `\_` escape in a BigQuery string literal, two spellings of "unknown" coexisting in one country column, and a cron line sharing a minute with the content-engine publish job while both `git pull`ed the same clone. *The transferable one: the Postgres half was tested against a real throwaway Postgres and was clean first try; the BigQuery half was never parsed by BigQuery and broke on its first run —* ***a pass that verifies one half thoroughly and the other not at all reports as "verified" and is not.*** The first five near-miss rows then forced a design change: **one is a 30-word LLM prompt someone typed into Google**, at position 6.2, which the research agent would have read as a topic — so both near-miss views now carry `word_count`, deliberately not as a filter. **Four stale claims elsewhere in this doc and three in `infrastructure.html` were pointed at this addendum in the same pass**, including the "future GSC pulls" and "GA4 export could eventually sync" planning notes, which were describing something now built.)) *Same-day earlier entry (**§33 addendum — Gemini per-caller usage accounting**: there is no AI Studio MCP and it would not help — **five consumers share one API key**, so Google can attribute spend to the key and never to the caller. `model_usage` added to the content schema, written by `call_model()`, with thinking tokens stored separately because `in + out ≠ total` on 3.x and the gap is billed as output. **The finding inverted the hypothesis**: the research agent is the only weekly job in the engine and its *smallest* consumer (~25k tokens, one call); the heaviest day observed is a **Saturday**, driven by **translate**; the whole engine spans ~8k–61k tokens/day. **The two consumers that can actually explain a weekly spike — the Monday 03:00 Hermes check-in and Iván's own Hermes sessions — do not go through `call_model` and remain unmeasured.**) *Previous entry, August 29, 2026 (**§9 addendum — GSC + GA4 into analytics Postgres**: the mechanism settled as *two opposite answers, one per source* rather than the hybrid the Aug 12 note assumed — **Search Console via the Search Analytics API for both backfill and nightly feed**, because the BigQuery bulk export is verifiably forward-only and choosing it would forfeit the 16 months that satisfy the feedback agent’s “60–90 days” trigger on day one; **GA4 via the BigQuery export already running**, because the property and its export were created the same day, so the export covers its entire life and needs no new credential, API or IAM grant. Four tables, five views, geography kept in the grain of all of them. Two decisions made unrepresentable rather than remembered: position is stored **additively** so averaging an average is impossible, and `analytics_sync_log` exists so a dead cron and a genuine zero cannot be confused. The one deliberate departure from `sync_pixel_data.py`: `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` over a rolling window, because GSC **revises** recent days and `DO NOTHING` would freeze each one at its first incomplete version — every row present, every number quietly low. ## Closed — September 11, 2026 (cleanup pass: the repo stops diagnosing the repo)

Session: Iván asked for an honest review of the whole repo, then for a cleanup so that documentation stopped being about documentation. Measured before the pass: `open-loops.md` 187 KB with a 13 KB session-narrative header, 102 open items averaging 1.8 KB in NOW/NEXT, 214 alert emoji, 15 items about the repo itself; `ai-infrastructure-documentation.md` "Last updated" line 44 KB; `triaperformance-project-instructions.md` 53 KB; `kb-hygiene-prompt.md` 31 KB. After: `open-loops.md` header five lines, every item four lines or fewer (pre-cleanup file snapshotted verbatim above); instructions and hygiene prompt rewritten; a writing rule added to the instructions and to project memory. Nothing was deleted from the record — three snapshots sit above this entry.

Five repo-about-the-repo items closed in the pass:

- [x] **The inventory tables are not covered by the standing check (three-time failure)** and **Generate `triaperformance-business-overview.md` §Interactive tools from `library.json` or delete the table** — closed together. Both hand-maintained tables (`triaperformance-business-overview.md` §Interactive tools and `artifact-publish-runbook.md` §Published artifacts) were deleted and replaced by a pointer to `site/_data/library.json`, which the build renders and which had been right every time. Option "delete and make the JSON the inventory of record" taken; no table to drift, nothing to add to the weekly pass.
- [x] **`ai-infrastructure-documentation.md`'s "Last updated" line** — replaced with one line. The 44 KB tail is in the snapshot above; the numbered sections hold the same history.
- [x] **The project's Custom Instructions field goes stale by design** — the index is now one line per doc and the whole instructions file is ~14 KB, so a repaste is a twenty-second act after the weekly pass (unconditional, step "END OF SESSION" in the checklist). The pointer-only design was not taken: an index that loads automatically is worth the weekly paste, and nothing had tested whether a session reliably follows a pointer.
- [x] **Decide what `members-exercise-dedup-review.md` is** — a generated report, superseded evidence, regenerable with `automation/cluster-exercise-duplicates.js`. Indexed as such; not open work.

Also this session: the 42 km race set (19 races / 26 pages) went live; the 21 km set was parked by Iván with the research finished ("I need to work on acquisition and retention"). September revenue and churn figures were deliberately not logged — they belong to close #2.
